I've been trying to ask the government what the consequence of this emissary of soldiers to an advanced position near the Russian border can be. The answer from the government has been that it cannot be ruled out that NATO's envoy will be followed by further Russian escalation. Nor can the government rule out that additional Danish soldiers will be sent if this arms race continues. In other words, we are currently in a spiral of escalation and armaments on which the government does not know the end. It's dangerous. That will undoubtedly strengthen the Russian President Putin and, at worst, it will end in an incalculable conflict. That is why Enhedslisten rejects the proposal from the government. We want pressure on President Putin; we do not want a strengthening of President Putin. We want political and economic pressure, not a headless armament and a arms race close to Denmark.

ParlaMint-DK_20170523134522	Thank you very much for the floor, sir. President. As draftsman of the opinion of the Committee on the Environment, Public Health and Consumer Protection, I would like to open this debate today. ParlaMint-DK_20170523134533	Thank you. Then we shall have the question answered, and that is the Prime Minister. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170523134543	Thank you so much. Today we will have the annual inquiry debate on the Kingdom Community, and I have been looking forward to it. It is a good tradition for us to gather together to discuss challenges and opportunities in the Kingdom community, and today it does not stop there, because later we will have a separate debate about Greenland's constitutional work and economic opportunities as an independent country raised by the Danish People's Party. I look forward to that too, and that is where we will have the opportunity to go into detail on those issues. The government wants a strong kingdom community, where we both stand together and allow room for diversity. We do this best through equal and trusting cooperation across geographical and political divides. Fortunately, this is how we feel today, both in areas which Denmark is responsible for for the whole Kingdom community, and if the Faroe Islands and Greenland so wish, also in the areas that have been taken over. The framework for our cooperation is, after all, the autonomy arrangements. These are systems that are modern and flexible, in which the Faroe Islands and Greenland have the opportunity to take over further competence and develop self-determination at the rate they want. Virtually all important decisions about the Faroese and Greenlandic communities are taken today in the Faroe Islands and in Greenland, and of course that is the case. This can legally be done because the Constitution offers very wide scope for self-determination. But in some areas it also sets limits. This is true, for example. in foreign and security policy. The Faroe Islands and Greenland cannot pursue an independent foreign and security policy, regardless of the kingdom of which they are part. This does not mean that the Faroe Islands and Greenland have been deprived of an international voice. Of course they are not, because we have also found constructive solutions in the field of foreign policy. This is true, for example. our work in the Arctic Council, where we have found practical solutions so that all three parts of the empire have the opportunity to speak out, and I think that is a good arrangement, and that makes good sense. I would also like to make it quite clear today that I respect the desire of the Faroe Islands and Greenland to be able to represent themselves as an independent and independent party to foreign policy, and especially where the interests of the Faroe Islands and Greenland intersect, such as Greenland. in the area of fisheries. I actually think it applies to all of us that there is respect for it. This is just not possible within the framework of the Constitution, and no Danish government can go beyond the Constitution. I think we will be the longest if we use our energy in dialogue with each other and work together to find practical solutions that take the interests of the Faroe Islands and Greenland as much as possible. That's my approach to it. I am fully aware that this approach does not respond to the Faroe Islands' wish for the current authorisation system to be extended, so that the Faroe Islands have more independent foreign policy options, but the fact is that it is not possible within the framework of the Basic Law, which means that the Kingdom is, in the legal and international sense, one subject of international law. On that basis, I have therefore invited you to sit down together and look at how to optimise the scope for action and minimise conflicts within the legal framework in force, and we are prepared to clarify and flesh out the conditions of the authorisation system together. It is still my hope, and I would like to repeat it here today, that the Faroe Islands will help to find the pragmatic solutions in this matter, so that we avoid major conflicts on the day of the finding of common solutions that concern both the Faroe Islands and Greenland. Also in relation to Brexit, the government attaches importance to continuous and close cooperation in, among others. to identify the consequences for the Faroe Islands and Greenland. The Faroe Islands and Greenland await important negotiations with the United Kingdom, where the fishing area is not least central, and we are happy to assist if the Faroe Islands or Greenland so wish. Both Greenland and the Faroe Islands work with their own constitutions. As I have said, Greenland's constitutional work will have to be discussed in greater depth later today, so I will confine myself to saying that the Greenland Constitutional Commission has received its mandate and has just begun its work. It has done so without at this stage being fully clear whether the Commission is only preparing proposals for a constitution to apply to an independent Greenland, or also a proposal to apply while Greenland is part of the Kingdom Community. I'll get back to that. The work of the Faroe Islands with its own constitution has been going on for a long time, and the national government has not yet completed its work on the draft Constitution. However, the lagman has recently indicated that a Faroese constitution will have the character of an identity document within the framework of the Kingdom Community, and it is not intended that the Faroe Islands should leave the Kingdom community with the Constitution. The lager has promised that there will be a referendum on the Constitution on 25 April next year, on the flag day of the Faroe Islands. I respect the desire for such an identity document, but it must be possible to accommodate it within the framework of the Constitution with the concerns and limitations that follow as long as the Faroe Islands are part of the Commonwealth. The government wants a good dialogue on these issues so that it can hopefully succeed in achieving the goal of the work in a way that is both satisfactory for the Faroe Islands and respects the constitution of the kingdom. When I want it, it is because we want the best for the Faroe Islands. That is why I am also pleased that the Faroe Islands have made great economic progress since 2013. There has been growth and it has been driven mainly by increasing exports of fish and fish products. Unemployment in the Faroe Islands now stands at only 2.3 per cent, and there is a very high level of activity, especially in the construction sector, which means that the national government expects a good surplus on public finances this year and the next few years. That is obviously positive. However, this does not change the fact that there is still a significant need for consolidation of the public economy. The Faroe Islands' Economic Council has assessed the need for budget improvements of 750 million. kr., or if you put it in relation to the Faroese economy, which is equivalent to 5 per cent. munity, in order to ensure a balance between revenue and expenditure in the medium term. As far as Greenland is concerned, we have discussed the economic situation several times in recent years here in the Folketing. Greenland faces quite major economic challenges in terms of growth, employment and fiscal sustainability, but also in terms of education and social problems. The Greenland Economic Council has for a number of years explained the issues in their reports. At present, the business cycle is good, but that does not change the fact that there is still a very large task in balancing public revenue and expenditure when there are more older people and when there are fewer people to earn money. The Greenland Economic Council points out that if development continues, public expenditure will exceed revenue by almost 1 billion. kr., or equivalent to more than 7%. of gross domestic product, in 2035. 1 billion kr. is a very large amount. Of course, the government respects the division of powers of the Self-Government Act, which means that Greenland itself is responsible for economic policy, to carry out necessary reforms in time and to create an economic foundation for welfare. Reforms and tightenings I know all about this is rarely popular, but in my optics there is a need for Greenland to take several steps in the right direction. We cannot rely solely on new revenue or invest our way out of the problems. This is something that the Greenland Economic Council has also pointed out. Having said that, the development of industry is, of course, part of the answer to the challenges, and we are therefore among others. ready to cooperate to attract funding for socio-economically sustainable business projects. We have, for example. made it possible for the Growth Fund in Denmark to provide co-financing to companies in Greenland, and the Export Credit Fund can guarantee the purchase of Danish products by Greenlandic projects. I know that a few weeks ago there was a major business conference in Nuuk, Future Greenland, where one of the main themes was precisely the attraction of capital for Greenland. Both politicians and the people of Greenland focus on investments in airports. The idea is, among other things, that more and better airports can increase tourism, which in turn can contribute to growth and new jobs. If you have traveled in Greenland, you have also experienced, as I have done, that you had to interstate in Kangerlussuaq. It is a fantastic place, but it does not change the fact that it increases and prolongs the journey if you have to fly over 300 km south again, for example. for a meeting in Nuuk, which most visitors have to do in a professional context. This corresponds to the fact that travelers who were going to a meeting in Denmark's capital, Copenhagen, from Europe had to interstate in Aalborg to come to Copenhagen. From Greenland's point of view, very large and complex investments are being made at new airports in several places in Greenland. The design and financing plans are being drawn up and it is therefore not yet possible to fully clarify all aspects. In his coalition agreement, Naalakkersuisut refers to a desire for the creation of a joint Danish-Greenland Development Fund for investments in profitable construction and infrastructure projects in Greenland, and I would like to state here that the government is naturally prepared to discuss the question if the Home Rule Government of Greenland is addressing itself. The service contract at Thulebasen is an important matter for both Greenland and Denmark, which we cooperate closely on. We have a common desire for rapid clarification, but this is a very complex matter, which has also been the subject of lengthy legal proceedings in the United States. The Foreign Minister and his Greenlandic colleague Mr Suka K. Frederiksen discussed the matter with the US Foreign Minister, Rex Tillerson, in connection with a ministerial meeting of the Arctic Council on 11 May. These are confidential negotiations and it is vital that we allow the negotiations to reach a satisfactory result. Camp Century: Here we have discussed a number of initiatives with Naalaksuisut, who would like to see the initiatives set in motion, and Danish authorities are in dialogue with the Greenland authorities about the further organisation. Researchers estimate that there will be decades before there is a risk of leakage of contaminated melt water at Camp Century, and therefore there is also time to organise appropriate environmental action. We have a strong tradition of cooperating in the Kingdom community. It is also a strong tradition to work together on the difficult issues, and we have a strong tradition of finding solutions to the challenges we face together. Cooperation and solidarity are the key words that describe the core of the Kingdom community, an open and equal dialogue is the way forward, and I believe that all three countries in the Kingdom community are the strongest when we stick together. Our cooperation and unity can only work because we speak openly and honestly with each other and because we respect each other's differences and preconditions. That is why I am looking forward to a good and constructive debate here today. Thank you for the floor.

In Greenland, the process has indeed been set in motion to the extent that, unfortunately, important political issues are neglected. However, it is to be welcomed that the so-called Commission of Reconciliation ends up as what one could foresee it would end up as a blue light. I understand that three out of the five in the Commission are said to have gone and no Danish ministers will waste time on it. It cost 10 million, by the way. kr. to implement. Unfortunately, however, certain Greenlandic politicians, such as Mrs Aleqa Hammond, who were the initiator of the Commission for Reconciliation, have succeeded in doing so, Mr. Lars-Emil Johansen, Hans Enoksen and others to make large parts of Greenland's young people feel that the Greenlanders are victims and the Danes are their executioners; that the Greenlanders are a oppressed people. I meet these young people both here and in Greenland and always get deeply frustrated to hear them crying and whining in the same way as their political models instead of fresh and cowardly addressing the country's problems. And sometimes I want to say to them: " Get a grip! ' However, it is not only Greenlanders who seem to have jumped on it with the victim and hangman. There are also many Danish politicians who are falling over each other and their own legs to stand up at all times apologetic and hand-wringing to pay for all the injustices that Denmark has allegedly caused to Greenland. But I have to say here from this platform that if that is how you want to lie to your people to get them to demand independence, then I think it is outrageous. If you achieve independence by making your people despise Denmark and the Danish people, then I think it is both outrageous and inexpensive, unworthy of the kingdom community, unworthy of the Greenlandic Landsting and local government. I and the Danish People's Party have often been criticised for interfering in Greenland's policy. And yes, it is absolutely right that I do so at all times and with great joy. I am strongly indignant about things that are going on in Greenland, among other things. the miserable social situation which exists on the island in many places and for which autonomy has full responsibility. At the same time, I am angry at the nepotism of Greenland's policy, which favours those who creep before the national government; that one disregards critics of the system; and not least how one seeks to silence cumbersome news media. I believe, in fact, that I have the full right, indeed the duty, to react in these cases, partly because Greenland is made up of 55,000 Danish citizens, partly because Danish taxpayers are very much financing the enterprise. So I consider it my duty as an elected official to interfere in Greenlandic and Faroese politics whenever I want and as much as possible. In this connection, let me mention that I was in Britain three years ago in the days surrounding the Scottish independence vote, when I had many discussions with Scottish and British politicians. The question to which the electorate in Scotland should vote 'yes' or 'no' to was in all its harsh simplicity: should Scotland be an independent country? In other words, should the United Kingdom, which has actually existed since 1746, be dissolved? And I was thinking that an even older union, namely the Danish-Norwegian Atlantic Empire and later the kingdom of Denmark between Denmark and the Faroe Islands and Greenland, is also threatened with dissolution, and that a vote in Greenland similar to that in Scotland could also easily become topical. But there is a crucial and dramatic difference in the way politicians in Denmark look at this matter and how politicians in England and Scotland act. If you ask an overwhelming majority of Christiansborg's politicians, and if you ask changing governments, the answer is usually: If the Greenlanders want independence, we should not interfere with it, it is Greenland's own business. On the other hand, if you ask an overwhelming majority of Westminster politicians how they view Scottish independence, the answer will be: Keep the Union, preserve the Union, we must and must interfere. And then millions of pounds were raised to campaign in Scotland, because the Union had to and had to be preserved. In Westminster, therefore, it is not the least regrettable to be a supporter of the Union and to be ready to fight for the Union. But here in the Folketing it is considered doubtful that, quote, interfere with the decision of the Greenlanders as it is expressed. As if we Danes were not a member of the kingdom of Denmark. And in Westminster, across the parties, a strong, united unity was created precisely under the slogan Keep the Union, and politicians from England, Scotland, Wales and Northern Ireland, during this slogan, carried out thunderspeaks in Scotland up to the referendum. However, I am almost convinced that if the Greens vote were to come, the most sensitive politicians in the Danish parliament would be guaranteed to stay in all kinds of places other than Greenland. They will say that they are in favour of the Kingdom community, because we hear this every time, but they will never actively engage in the struggle to preserve the Kingdom community with their beaks and claws. They could easily seem colonial. In England, therefore, there is little embarrassment about fighting, advertising, advertising, speaking and writing in Scotland to preserve the union. It should be mentioned in this connection that the Scottish Nationalist Party's rotating presidents, Alex Salmond and Nicola Sturgeon, bid with open arms and warmly welcomed the British Union's supporters to Scotland for debate. I doubt that the Greenlandic politicians would look the same way at the matter that Salmond and Sturgeon are doing. There would probably be some who would welcome it, but the majority would be greatly outraged by the presence of goose-eyes in Danish interference. That is why I must stress once again that we Danes are therefore also a member of the Kingdom of Denmark. So if the accident should happen and a vote on Greenlandic or Faroese independence lies in favour, then it is my intention to call members of the Folketing to a founding meeting of the Keep the Union, or perhaps better preserve the kingdom. Then, across the parties, we can gather millions together for the campaign we are about to run in Greenland, and equip ourselves to participate actively in the campaign in Greenland as well as in the Faroe Islands prior to the vote. I can barely wait. Then why? Well, that's why we have a unique community built on history, tradition and trust, plus the fact that we are and become countrymen. Thank you.

That is the way I feel at least, and I can hear that Mr. Søren Espersen is not satisfied with it and that you fight for the greatest possible independence and democracy for all people. I will support that and then it does not matter whether they live in Catalonia, in the Basque Country, in Western Sahara or in Palestine. It is now such a principle that I have, and I think that is a good principle. I am also in favour of decentralizing power in Denmark, for example, regardless of whether Mr. Søren Espersen could do campaigns for that reason. That is why I also support those people in Greenland who aim to be independent. I think it's a very, very nice and honorable goal that you can be master of your own house and decide for yourself. If I were to make a few critical points about the development in Greenland and where I would like to make a call to the politicians in Greenland, it is especially in relation to the problems that are still absent with socially vulnerable children and young people in Greenland. It is not acceptable that the situation is such and we have to repeat that if Greenland thinks that there is something that Denmark can do financially or in terms of resources, then we have to do so. Up to one third of Greenlandic children should not live in miserable conditions or be abused or otherwise have a life that is not proper. It does not belong in the Kingdom community, and that is why we have to make such good offers that Greenland uses those offers and that we work our way out of that situation. Then there is the inequality in Greenland. As a socialist, I am in favour of combating inequality, and I can see that inequality in Greenland is rapidly increasing. There is a very, very large difference between the poorest in the settlements and the richest in Nuuk, who have made sure that they have a good life that is fully equal to the Danish one. And it is important to pay attention to this, because although there are economic problems in the country, it is important that we also work on the issue of inequality. The second point I would like to make is, of course, the big question of uranium. I and the Enhedslist will support Greenlanders who think that policy should be pursued with zero tolerance, which means that uranium is not being dug up. We have enough problems in the world with nuclear bombs and nuclear weapons, as they actually call it modernisation, and there is no need for more uranium to be delivered to the world market. On the contrary, I would like our government instead of helping the United Nations to ban the nuclear weapons that some countries have fortunately taken responsibility for trying to make a new decision in the United Nations. Unfortunately, the Danish chair is empty. Another issue is the Thule contract, which has been dealt with. I think it is important that we say: Now we will soon have something concrete on the table. I do not have much optimism about the government's ability to find a good solution to this. I think the government's gonna have to get the wallet because this is gonna end badly. Nor is there much to suggest that the Americans are giving in here. The very last story is that it seems that the Royal Arctic Line is now losing transports to Thule; it seems that the Americans will take them home. Although Royal Arctic Line can do it for half price, the Americans say they have more confidence that an American company also stands for deliveries to Thule. I think that is very, very problematic and I would like to hear the Minister's opinion on what the hell we should do about it. Fortunately, Camp Century has become visible to a lot of people, and I think it is important that we push here. I do not think there is any reason for the Prime Minister, as he said in his oral report, to wait because it may well take 10 years before the low-radioactive waste begins to seep with the melt water to places where it can do great damage. I think we should try to make some arrangements about what we need to clean up and how we can do it, and then we have to figure out how to pay the bill and take the problems in terms of how serious they are. Well, some rusty barrels at Tasiilaq may not be as big a problem to tackle as this low-radioactive waste, which is why it is important that there is an overview and a priority. Then I would also like to point to the municipal officers. I think it is important that we continue to work on this. I actually feel that having one municipal bailiff in a village must be hard. I mean, if some people went a little crazy on a Saturday night, maybe it was nice to be two to do that job, and then you had to make sure that in all the settlements there was a place where you could make them cool down again. We must continue to work on this problem, as must the police in Greenland. We all know that there has been work on proper telephone connections so that you can actually contact each other all over Greenland. That is also a problem. Then I would say that in the Faroe Islands things are improving immediately and with a 50 per cent increase in revenue. From 2012 to 2016, first of all from fish, well then it is almost a luxury problem we are talking about, and therefore I understand that confidence is great in the Faroe Islands. I also understand that two new tunnels need to be built now, so there is also work for those building workers who may have skills in this direction. And I think that's going quite well, so there's not much that can beat a fucking stick right in these years. That is why the whole debate on the constitutions is also important. I dream of a situation where we will have a kingdom with three independent kingdoms or three independent states. It would be a real kingdom community. I don't think you should cry about it if it's the form we're going to reach at some point. On the contrary, it will be much more equal than it is today. That is why I find the work of the Constitution exciting, and I will be very happy to follow it. And I have no imagination to imagine how it should develop, so that it is contrary to the Constitution. I don't think that talk is content. If the Prime Minister wants to continue with that talk, he has to explain how the initiatives in the Faroe Islands and Greenland can be contrary to the Danish constitution when we have an agreement with them that they can become self-employed if they wish. In principle, this cannot be contrary to the Danish constitution. I therefore look forward to how the two countries will formulate their new constitutions. I think we might even be inspired by it. Since none of them seem to be planning to form kingdoms, I will look in particular at how they will deal with the alternative to a king. I think this is really, really exciting, because it could be that in Denmark we could get inspiration to form the Republic of Denmark. After all, international agreements are important, and it is important that we continue to work intensively to ensure that Faroese and Greenlanders sit at as many tables as possible every time it has to do with their territory. We must also respect the fact that there are sometimes different interests between Greenland and the Faroe Islands and that we must therefore try to find a way of incorporating it when we negotiate internationally, both in relation to quotas, but also in relation to all the other things. Then the Faroese would like to be a member of the Nordic Council. I think that is a good idea and I have supported it in the Nordic Council. I think that Denmark should make a great effort to say that of course they can be a full member of the Nordic Council of Ministers and the Nordic Council. That would be very sensible, especially in the form of the Nordic Council. There's no reason they're not. Then there are the climatic conditions. It is extremely important that we work everywhere, and especially in Arctic cooperation, to preserve the climate that exists, but also to make the Arctic nuclear weapons free. It's crazy that we have such unstable presidents with a finger so close to the red button that could trigger a disaster. I am not only thinking of Trump, I am also thinking of Putin, and I am also thinking of Pakistan and Israel. There are plenty of countries that should not have nuclear weapons, and we should simply make sure that they were gone. There we could start with the North, and we could start with the Arctic and say that we will have no nuclear weapons there. If we did that, we would take a huge cross-border step. Now I didn't get to talk about the defense and the police and the continental shelf and the charts, which aren't finished yet. I think it's a scandal. But I'm sure we'll talk about that sometime. I have a proposal for adoption which I would like to read out: Proposal for adoption The Danish Parliament considers that an equal and respectful cooperation between Denmark, the Faroe Islands and Greenland benefits all three countries in the Kingdom Community. The Folketing recognises that the Faroe Islands and Greenland are free to work with their own constitutions. The Danish Parliament urges the government to stay informed about the economic challenges facing the Faroe Islands and Greenland in ensuring sustainable and sustainable social development and contributing actively to the promotion of investment in all parts of the Kingdom Community. The Danish Parliament notes an increased international interest in the Arctic and the North Atlantic and calls for increased efforts to benefit all parties and for cooperation on foreign and security policy between Denmark, the Faroe Islands and Greenland to continue to be a priority in accordance with the principles of the Fámjin and Itilleq Declarations and the Kingdom of Denmark's strategy for the Arctic 2011-2020. In order to promote the development of the Kingdom Community, Parliament calls for cooperation between the three countries to be strengthened and continued to be given priority. • V 111).

Basically, we think that we should have much more focus on knowledge about the kingdom community, knowledge about culture, in our own country, Denmark, and in teaching in the country. One point which is mentioned only very fleetingly in the statement, or which could at least have been given more focus, is climate change and, above all, their enormous impact on Greenland and on the Greenlandic population. Ice and permafrost melt, threaten infrastructure and affect life in Greenland. There are many examples. I do not think anyone in this Chamber is in any doubt that the seriousness of man-made climate change is pushing globally, and not least in Greenland. The decommissioned American base, Camp Century, which has been mentioned several times, is another example of what it can mean when the ice melts. Despite the fact that DF's rapporteur believes that this is not something we need to take a position on right now, it is therefore environmentally hazardous waste which is 30-75 metres below the ice and which can be exposed and perhaps even leaked out with waste water within a few years. I still think it is necessary to establish whether Camp Century is a defence area in relation to the 1951 agreement and its rules on waste or not. And if the government believes that Camp Century is a defence area, when was it and by what rules? How much time does the government really need to spend on resolving the legal issues of clean-up responsibilities? It's been going on for almost a year now. How long is this gonna take? I'd like to get the PM to answer that. We often hear that climate change is not going to affect us very much here in Denmark, but when part of the Kingdom community is as directly affected as Greenland already is, it should be another argument for Denmark to take the lead when it comes to a green, sustainable and ambitious climate policy. In fact, I find it embarrassing that these challenges are not taken seriously. In this context, it is gratifying to see that there are so many research projects and collaborations taking place among the Nordic countries in the Arctic Council and in the Kingdom Community. The temperature rises twice as fast in the Arctic as the global average temperature, the sea ice is receding, and conditions are changing for the peoples of the Arctic. At the same time, new shipping routes, new opportunities for mining and fishing, call for an urgent need to find solutions: monitoring the consequences of increased activity in the Arctic, monitoring pollution and new agreements that ensure people in the Arctic and the Arctic environment. Again, I would like to commend the great scientific work being done, including in Greenland, to create the best possible knowledge base so that we politicians have something to act on and speak from. A few good projects that I personally have come across and want to highlight are Greenland Perspective, which explores the possibilities of using nutrient-rich mud from Greenland to tormented soils around the world. This is a good example of how the unique environment in Greenland and the Arctic can be used to create better conditions, a better world. There are also other projects including extraction of unique enzymes that have interesting perspectives. However, it is important to stress that when companies and researchers extract minerals and raw materials in the Arctic, including Greenland, this must be done in respect of the environment and people. Basically, it is clear that if you want to help Greenland, it will not be possible to relax your ambitions in relation to global climate action. Therefore, I do not understand that when the government and DF talk about the fact that we would very much like to have a warm and strong kingdom community and strong parties within this kingdom community, it is not being wagered and that it is not being recognised that then the Arctic and Greenland's climate challenges are central to Denmark. In the alternative, we have a lot of proposals on how to meet these climate challenges; proposals that are often shot down by the government, despite the fact that these proposals also benefit the Kingdom community and the challenges that climate change brings. I am more lacking in the fact that the disastrous development we see in the Arctic is recognised as a global problem, that is, as our problem and not a local problem in the Arctic. Our behavior, including Denmark, has a direct impact on the warming in the Arctic. Therefore, consideration of the Arctic must be given to all our policies. It is not enough to initiate isolated initiatives. We need a radical change in our production and consumption patterns. Denmark should not use its influence in the Arctic to ensure that we just have our narrow interests taken into account. We should take the lead in stopping the heating of the Arctic. I would therefore like us to give more active support to sustainable development projects and also to socially and environmentally sustainable mining and the development of renewable energy sources. We must ensure that development in Greenland and our investments help ensure sustainable development for people and nature. I would also have liked to see the report focus more on the social and economic conditions of the community's inhabitants. It is no secret, as Enhedslisten's rapporteur also says, that certain parts of the community have massive social and economic challenges. We need to focus more on them. As I asked the DF's rapporteur to comment, we also have challenges at home in Denmark, and I do not believe that this is the responsibility of the individual citizen. I also believe that the state should take the lead in ensuring that we have an enlightened population that takes into account the parties who travel here from the Kingdom Community. It is essential for the alternative that we ensure that the Arctic remains a low-voltage area. I am therefore pleased that the government is stressing the importance of the Ilulissat Declaration. We are not interested in great political rivalry and geopolitical interest, let alone in the fact that this will happen at the expense of the local population and, above all, at the expense of the climate. It is therefore also positive that the European Union has now drawn up a genuine Arctic policy which, among other things, includes: focuses on climate change and on sustainable development. I hope that the government will take this into account when drawing up its new international strategy and foreign policy. Finally, I would like to know how the government specifically envisages the Paris Agreement in cooperation within the Kingdom Community. And also in the context of the new foreign policy: how do we involve concrete representatives of the other countries of the Kingdom to provide input, or how do we ensure that they are constantly consulted in this process? There is no doubt that the development of the Arctic and the Kingdom Community creates both new challenges and new opportunities, and we hope that Denmark will maintain a course towards creating economic, social and environmental sustainable development with respect for both indigenous peoples and peoples. Thank you for the floor.

ParlaMint-DK_20170523163628	Thank you very much. Empire means fellowship in the kingdom. Community is called in Greenlandic ataatsimoorneq. It means you're one with each other, being one about everything in the community. Do I feel as a Greenlandic citizen that I am treated on an equal footing with everyone else in the whole kingdom? Will Danish affairs be given the same priority in Greenland as they are given here in Denmark? Do we have the same legal certainty? Is it the Danish language, the family ties or anything else that connects me with the rest of the kingdom community? Yes, many questions about the Kingdom community can be asked about political matters. The Prime Minister repeatedly stresses that it is in Danish interest to keep the Kingdom Community and thus Greenland. The Taksøe report highlighted that it is in Danish interest to hold on to the Kingdom community. The Self-Government Act clearly states that Greenland has the right to independence. Greenland decides for itself. The challenges of the Kingdom community have not diminished in time. On the contrary, I believe that the Kingdom is in rapid change, a very clear change. The Self-Government Act came into force in 2009 and thus the relationship between Denmark and Greenland has changed. Greenland has the right to the underground, the Greenlandic language is now the official language. We can decide for ourselves when we want independence. The Self-Government Act has set very clear guidelines for the roadmap for the independence process. This also means that our relations with each other, both in Denmark and Greenland, at least on the political level, have changed, a change that the absolute majority in Greenland wants, namely that we are working towards independence. We now have independence, and the constitutional work has been initiated. The wishes of the people are brought forward in the political work. A joint project and a shared responsibility. A strong community is a community where you feel equal value, and you share decisions on each other's affairs and take joint responsibility. Unfortunately, this is not always the community we feel when we from Denmark make decisions that affect Greenland's everyday life without having taken the Greenlandic community into the decisions. If the Kingdom community is to be preserved, Denmark must take its responsibility seriously. There are still 32 matters under Danish jurisdiction, which all do not fully meet today's needs and today's Greenland. With such great responsibility, it is not enough to say that one considers the Kingdom community important, nor is it enough to say that one wants a strong community. Empire is a commitment. It obliges Denmark to give priority to its responsibilities and that these also continue to improve like all other parts of the Kingdom community. Us, who live in the northern part of the Commonwealth, experience time and again that the Danish responsibilities are at the end of the list of priorities. So it does not exactly strengthen our sense of fellowship in the realm. If you want a strong Kingdom community, it requires constant effort to do so strongly and firmly. Efforts are being made to remove border barriers which hamper the smooth coexistence of cultural, gastronomic, linguistic and educational initiatives. Respect for each other's different history, culture, spirituality and belonging are a good starting point for a strong community. A community of kingdoms which we want at the political level and a community of kingdoms which we want on the social side can be seen in concrete initiatives. How much money is being spent on ensuring that our young people are taught factual realities about each other's social conditions? How much do our young people really know about each other's countries? For example, I have. Here in the house had a lot of high school students visiting, young people who wanted to know a little about Greenlandic conditions and about relations between Denmark and Greenland. I see a very little knowledge about Greenland in young people, unfortunately, but a very great desire not to see Greenland as independent. I believe that a strong kingdom is a community that is united in respect for each other's different views, different cultures and different priorities. There I would say that the Kingdom community will be built on a community with solid knowledge of each other, of each other's affairs and of each other's values. The government base gives a hope for a better policy for matters in Greenland, which is under Danish responsibility. That legal certainty must be at the same level as it is in Denmark is among the priorities of the government base. These are major objectives, and this requires strong cooperation between Greenland and Denmark. And the two who met there were Harald Bluetooth, who gathered Denmark in his time, and Tróndur í Gøtu, the most famous Faroese Viking chief ever. Tróndur solved the big problem that Harald Bluetooth came into when he was robbed of all his silver in Elsinore. He refused all merchants to leave Elsinore again before he had got the silver back, but they didn't find out who had stolen it. So what did Tróndur do? Yes, he negotiated with all the merchants that they each paid a small sum of silver, and then he delivered it to Harald Bluetooth. This was pleased, the merchants were allowed to sail, and Tróndur received a splendid salary for his work and sailed back to the Faroe Islands and fought for 50 years against the Norwegian kings for the freedom of the Faroe Islands. When I entered politics two years ago, it was not only to help develop a strong and independent Faroe Islands. The Faroe Islands are undergoing rapid development, and the responsibility is great, as Faroese taxpayers themselves finance 90%. of the Faroese state budget and the Latting itself are mostly part of the legislation. It is therefore of great importance for the cohesion of our societies that we should adopt a constitution which provides a common framework for our identity. One of the fundamental principles of a Faroese constitution is the confirmation of Faroese self-determination and that the Faroese people must be asked if significant agreements are to be reached that oblige our country in relation to other nations. The work on the Constitution is now entering a final phase, and the referendum is scheduled for 25 April 2018, which is also the Faroese flag day. Over the autumn and winter, the proposal must be presented and discussed both in the Latting and among the public before the vote, and I have great faith that the attendance at the vote will be great. About 90 percent. of they asked in a recent Gallup survey indicated that they would participate in a referendum. When the Danish Constitution was voted in 1953, the turnout was down to 7%. This clearly confirms a great interest in a Faroese constitution, which is a contract between the Faroese people and those who are given the task of managing power in society. In Denmark, the government has been less sympathetic to Faroese ambition. Rather, I find that the government is trying to shut down the dialogue with its defensive announcements that any change in relations between countries must remain within the framework of the constitution. It is therefore trying to limit political dialogue to a question of law. But here all dialogue is lost, because the Prime Minister's Office and the Ministry of Justice will always interpret the framework of the Constitution in relation to the Government's wishes. At the same time, when you claim that the Prime Minister and the Ministry of Justice are the only authoritative interpreters of the Constitution, you have effectively shut down any constructive political dialogue. It's too bad, and it's wrong. And this confirms the prejudices that the Danish state has difficulty in moving into dynamic and respectful cooperation between three independent parties and still clings to a late colonial ambition to exercise power over all three parts of the kingdom as long as possible. The gravity of Danish foreign policy is closely linked to the Arctic growth that Denmark can bring at negotiating tables around the world. So it takes some skill to navigate that environment. The Western Nordic Council includes parliamentary cooperation between Greenland, Iceland and the Faroe Islands, which focuses on our living conditions in the North Atlantic and the Arctic. I would also like to acknowledge that Denmark supports the Arctic people's right to make sustainable use of marine resources, which is a prerequisite for the welfare of the Arctic. And I hope that Denmark will also support a proposal that is about tightening up the conditions for membership as an observer in the Arctic Council. They must recognise the rights of the Arctic people to make sustainable use of marine resources, and countries with observer status must open their markets to all products derived from sustainable catches, including marine mammals. The EU's ban on imports of seal products has been a disaster for Arctic communities, and damage can only be limited by lifting the ban. When you can see that something is economically necessary, it is very often politically impossible to do so. When the problems become so obvious that you can see that it is a political necessity to do so, it is often too late economically. An area in which the Faroe Islands have acquired their own gradually stronger identity is in the field of foreign policy. Here, in his speech today, the Prime Minister said that the Constitution sets clear limits. But it is not so rare for fisheries policy and trade policy to become foreign policy. This is done in intergovernmental negotiations on fisheries, and there is no doubt that Faroese exports to Russia have a foreign policy aspect. But although foreign policy is formally a common matter, the government's wise line is that it is not interfered with. When support for the Commonwealth is as large in the Faroe Islands as it is at present, it is not least due to this wise line. However, unlike the Prime Minister, I believe that we can go further. In his time, a Danish Prime Minister fully supported Faroese membership of the Nordic Council. Now it is said that this cannot be done, and refers to the Constitution of the same Constitution as it was then, when it was possible to do so. It is said that the Faroe Islands cannot have their own voice in international organizations if Denmark or Greenland are in, because the kingdom can only speak with one voice under the Constitution. The Prime Minister writes in today's chronicle in Jyllands-Posten and in Sosialurin that no Danish government can go beyond the constitution, even in the service of good will. Of course, but the scope for manoeuvre is very great when the political will is there. The Constitution is not, it has proven throughout history, a cage of steel. It's turned out to be like an accordion. And if it is to contain a community where everyone can feel right, then it must be pulled out completely. It is important to choose the politically pragmatic path that has led us so far together, rather than the legal path that so often causes us problems. Of course, what is at stake in the Community must be the political, economic, social and personal opportunities and not the legal constraints. That is why I will also sign for the cooperation I have had with the government to remove border barriers in the Kingdom community. For example: it is now possible to get a CPR number even if you live in the Faroe Islands. And forefathers in Denmark, who in recent years have had more difficult access to certain social benefits than other Danish nationals, are now treated equally. I also sign for the government's goodwill in relation to my proposals for co-financing of Danish-Færøske projects. I'm gonna sign up for the few flybones we've had since we last had this debate. Finally, I would like to say that what is going to make the Kingdom community work is not just the formal framework; there must also be a lively interest in it. And the prerequisite for interest is, of course, knowledge, and there is a slight lag here on land. Some material has been made about the Faroe Islands for school use, but it is mostly such small teasers. That is fine, but we should go further. Learning about the Faroe Islands should at least be a tiny bit of any Danish primary school student's regular curriculum. So, in summary, although certain things can be done better, the answer to the question put by the Prime Minister in today's newspaper must be that, yes, tað gongur fínt. Thank you.

ParlaMint-DK_20170523175013	Thank you. And we're gonna get it now. Prime Minister, please.
ParlaMint-DK_20170523175019	Thank you for that and thank you to the Danish People's Party for taking the initiative in this question debate on Greenland's constitutional work and economic opportunities as an independent country. It is true that we have at least had elements of the debate on the table already in the question debate that we have just concluded. But here you can then, you might say, zoom in more directly on exactly this question which interests a lot of people in Greenland, but also here in Denmark. It is, of course, a question of a constitution that has been under way for some time, and in November 2016, Inatsisartut decided, on the basis of a statement by the Naalaksuisut, to authorise the national government to set up a Greenlandic Constitutional Commission and to draw up a mandate for the work. On 26 April, a member of the Naalaksuisut Suka K. Frederiksen informed me of the mandate which has now been drawn up as the basis for the work of the Commission. In principle, the Commission is working with a time frame of 2-3 years, and the members of the Commission must as a rule be members of the Landstinget. The Commission consists of seven members and all parties have proposed members to the Commission, and Vivian Motzfeldt from Siumut is chairman. Both the Naala Suisut and the Commission may appoint members of the Commission and the President of the Commission may invite other experts to take part in the work. The Commission shall organise its own work, establish a secretariat to serve the Commission. According to the terms of reference, the Commission is to draw up proposals for a Greenlandic constitution in two stages. It states that some must be able to enter into force while Greenland is part of the Kingdom and, in the case of Greenland, must supplement the Basic Law, while the second part must enter into force only on the day Greenland becomes independent. That is clear from the terms of reference. I understand, however, that there has subsequently been a discussion between the Member of the Naalaksuisut Suka K. Frederiksen, the Commission Presidency and the Inatsisartut ad hoc committee on the Constitutional Commission on the detailed understanding of the mandate, a discussion on whether the Commission is only preparing proposals for a constitution to apply to an independent Greenland, or also a proposal to apply while Greenland is part of the Kingdom Community. The Commission held its first meeting on 15 May and issued a press release shortly ago. It appears that the Commission has confirmed here that it will work according to a constitution that will apply when Greenland becomes an independent state, and so I have to say that, from my desk chair and now from the platform, there is currently not full clarity about the work of the Commission. I can also say that the press release of the Constitutional Commission shows that the Commission also discussed the question of how best to bring the people together, information on ownership of resources on land and on water, about space for diversity and equal opportunities and about the protection of living, values and languages as a basis for identity. In addition, it appears that the possibility of joining the United States as a recognised state on an equal footing with other states and NATO membership was also discussed. It is with reference to the press release. We'll follow the commission's work. I would like to mention today, as I have already indicated, that it is clear that the future Greenlandic Constitution must respect the Constitution if it is to apply while Greenland is part of the Kingdom Community, and that is why I am also pleased that the Terms of Reference stipulate that if the Commission draws up provisions to apply while Greenland is part of the Kingdom Community, it must involve the Ministry of Justice in order to ensure that the content of the draft Constitution can be assessed on an ongoing basis by the Ministry of Justice, so that it can be included within the framework of the Constitution. I would like to sign for that approach to cooperation by the Naala Suisut. I also expect a direct dialogue to be established between the member of the Naala Suisut for independence and the Minister for Justice if there is a need for discussions between the government and the national government along the way. It is the status of the constitutional work, as I can uncover it from here at the moment. I am also looking forward to the Council meeting that we will have before the Bornholm People's Assembly and hope that it will give me more insight into things than what I can refer to here. Then there is the question of the economy, which is central, because Greenland itself presupposes a self-sustaining economy, and with reference to the debate we have just had, I also think it is appropriate to discuss independence, independence, formality, reality. After all, we have discussed the economic situation in the past and some of the challenges that Greenland faces, and that is, to put it bluntly, not insignificant challenges. Greenland needs reforms and new revenue. Financial policy is not sustainable, that is to say, there is a good economic picture at the moment, and that is very good, but major budgetary improvements will have to be made in the coming years if welfare is to be maintained, as there are more elderly people and fewer people to earn money. And according to the Greenland Economic Council, the sustainability challenge amounts to up to 1 billion. kr. or more than 7% It is true, as the questioner said, that if we are to anticipate the figure in order to understand it in a Danish political context, it is equivalent to 100 billion. kr. in Denmark. This responds to the need to improve the budget in 2035 perspective by 100 billion. kr. to have durability. This is quite a challenge. Then there is the state subsidy to Greenland, that is the block subsidy, and it amounts to 3.7 billion this year. kr. This corresponds to just over half, 55%, of the total income of the Treasury, or in a more understandable way: For every 2 DKK that comes in the box, 1 DKK comes. from Denmark. It is then a grant that makes it possible for Greenland to have a significantly higher level of prosperity than would otherwise have been the case, i.e. without a Danish block grant. In addition, the state carries out a number of tasks in Greenland, which the autonomy has the possibility to take over, but which have not yet been taken over. In the last debate, we had a debate on the level at which we are taking care of it, and I also listened to the kind but certain criticisms made by some of the Greenlandic Members. Very topically, the Autonomy Act lays down 33 areas of action which have not been taken and which, among other things, are: I would also like to thank the rapporteur for his excellent report, which he has drawn up on behalf of the Committee on Economic and Monetary Affairs and Industrial Policy, and the Committee on Economic and Monetary Affairs and Industrial Policy. kr. So there is a block grant of 3.7 billion. kr., and then there are non-removed tasks that we spend about 0.5 billion. kr. at present. Of these, the cases under the Ministry of Justice amount to up to 400 million. kr. annual. It follows from the Autonomy Act that Greenland itself finances new fields of action which are taken over, and Greenland, of course, decides whether, after a takeover, more or less money will be spent in the areas than the state has done so far; I can just explain what we are currently spending. I also note what has been said about the fact that in some areas we, from the point of view of Greenland, could do better. Then there are some areas occupied by the government and which can only be transferred to Greenland by independence. This concerns, among other things: On foreign policy, security policy and defence policy, Denmark currently spends more than 0.5 billion. kr. annually on the defence's assignment in Greenland, and in particular on the supremacy of sovereignty. In addition, the defence also carries out a number of other social tasks, including: search and rescue teams and a large part of marine environment preparedness. And with the agreement to strengthen the future task solution of the Ministry of Defence in the Arctic from December 2016, an additional 120 million will be added. kr. annually to the area. It was just to draw a picture of the current economy, and without trampling around in a conclusion, I think it is quite quiet to see that there is a challenge in terms of having a total economic load with the level of prosperity and welfare that currently exists in Greenland. The question of which rights and obligations between Greenland and Denmark will apply under international law, if Greenland decides to be independent, was after all dealt with by the Greenlandic-Danish Self-Government Commission, it is reflected in the Self-Government Act, and here it is assumed that the implementation of autonomy for Greenland will take place by an agreement between the Government and the Naalaksususut, and such an agreement will have to decide on a number of issues as a result of international law. It is a question of, among other things: citizenship of persons affected by Greenland's independence. Under international law, however, there is no requirement for Denmark's financial support to be maintained in full or in part after Greenland has gained independence. I should like to mention that the government of that time, which we are back in the year 2000, offered an economic transitional period during which the State grant was paid out after four years in the course of negotiations with the Faroe Islands on independence. I am not going to stand here and anticipate the outcome of a debate, let alone an agreement on the implementation of Greenland's independence sometime in the future; it must be based on the specific circumstances prevailing at the time when it might become a reality, but I do not imagine a situation in which the state's grant or task solution will continue for a longer period after Greenland's independence. If a majority in Greenland decides for independence, the government will of course respect the decision, but we will regret it, and personally I will mourn it. It is my wish and hope that we will continue to be united in a strong Kingdom community for many years to come for the benefit of our peoples and for the benefit of the development of our society. In my optics, according to the last debate, the current system of self-government is unique, also in a historical perspective. It provides security for Greenlandic society, while at the same time Greenland has the opportunity to take over further competence and develop self-determination at the rate that Greenland itself wishes. And then it means that virtually all important decisions about Greenlandic society are taken in Greenland, and of course it must be so. As everyone knows, I am a great supporter of our historical kingdom community; I am convinced that it makes both Denmark and Greenland stronger, and I hope that we will love each other and stick together for many years to come. Thank you.

The crucial issue here is economics, and a Greenland on its own is a self-sustaining economy. There are three elements in this overall. Firstly, as I mentioned in my introduction, and as has also been the subject of today's debate, Greenland needs reforms and new revenue. Financial policy is not sustainable. According to the Greenland Economic Council, the sustainability challenge is therefore in the region of 1 billion. kr. or 7%. of GDP in 2035. This is a reality which, if necessary, must be addressed. Secondly, there is the state's contribution to Greenland, which is, of course, this 3.7 billion. kr. or just over half of the total income of the national treasury, which currently makes it possible to have a higher level of prosperity than would otherwise be possible and which, by its very nature, will also have to relate to the absence of, in a scenario of autonomy. Thirdly, there are the more than 30 areas which Greenland has the opportunity to take over, but which are still managed by the state, and where we currently have costs in the region of an estimated 0.5 billion. kr., where it follows from the Act of Self-Government that Greenland itself finances these matters if they are taken over. Finally, as I mentioned in my first speech, there are indeed those tasks which are the responsibility of the rich and which are currently being carried out in a way that is used in the region of 0.5 billion. kr. on the defence mission; it is strengthened with the Arctic effort we have agreed, which will be added an additional 120 million. kr. So it is certainly important that these things are kept in mind in relation to the further work. Having said that, I have the greatest respect for the fact that in Greenland you decide which political issues you want to focus your energy on. I only hope that the things I have mentioned here will be taken into account in the further work, and I will just repeat what I have said before, namely that, in my view, the current system of self-government is quite unique. It provides security for the Greenlandic community, which has challenges, while Greenland has the opportunity to take over further competence and develop self-determination at the rate that Greenland itself wants it to happen. So let me conclude by thanking everyone for a good and committed debate, not just about Greenland's future in this question debate, but about the Kingdom Community in general in the previous debate. It's been a couple of interesting hours.

ParlaMint-DK_20170523191729	Thank you for that, and I shall be very brief. On behalf of all the parties of the Danish Parliament, I shall ask the Minister for Climate Change about the future energy policy initiatives in the light of the energy policy statement that we have been circulated and read. ParlaMint-DK_20170523191800	Thank you to the rapporteur. And then it is the minister for reply. Here you go.
ParlaMint-DK_20170523191805	Thank you to the President and thank him. Jens Joel. I have been looking forward to the debate on the energy policy statement. This allows for a good debate on energy policy in Denmark. I believe that we in Denmark have long led an ambitious energy policy with very renewable energy in the system, high security of supply and focus on the development of green energy technology. The government will, of course, continue these tactics, and that is why we have set the ambitious goal that Denmark should have at least 50% by 2030. of its energy needs covered by renewable energy. The 2030 target is a good step towards the long-term goal that the Danish energy system should be independent of fossil fuels by 2050. But I would like to start a completely different place from domestic politics, which is in the world. It is clear that the Danish energy policy cannot be seen in isolation. We also need to move globally and, fortunately, there are also globally good bars. As the statement also shows, the world, with the Paris Agreement and the UN's 17 World Goals from 2015, has for the first time been given a global framework for the green transition. As responsible for two thirds of global greenhouse gas emissions, the energy sector is at the heart of this. Virtually all countries' climate targets include an increase in the use of renewable energy and increased efforts to promote energy efficiency. The transformation of the international energy system is therefore already under way. Renewable energy prices have fallen dramatically in recent years and in 2016 1261⁄2 gigawatt solar and wind energy was installed globally. These are impressive figures, and that is also a new record. The green transition is expected to accelerate in the coming years. This will increase international demand for green solutions and, at the same time, increase competition to deliver the energy technology of the future. Denmark is, fortunately, well equipped in that competition. In March, the government launched an overall energy export strategy, and this is the first of its kind in Danish history. The strategy is to ensure that Denmark remains a pioneer country and exploits its energy strengths. The ambitions are large and the goal is that we will at least double our energy technology and service exports by 2030. At the same time, Denmark continues to advise major emerging economies such as China, South Africa, Vietnam and Mexico on policies to promote renewable energy and energy efficiency. This work is an important Danish contribution to the global transformation. The cooperation works at the same time as a good showcase for Danish companies' green solutions, and therefore it also helps to increase Danish export opportunities. If we zoom a little into the big emerging economies, it is also quite clear that energy policy is at the heart of the European agenda. As late as November 2016, the Commission launched a comprehensive winter energy package. The package is a key part of the implementation of the EU Energy Union and covers everything from energy efficiency and energy efficiency in buildings to renewable energy, a new design of the electricity market and a management system for the Energy Union. The Winter Package is an important contribution to ensuring the EU's energy and climate objectives. The package must therefore help to ensure that the European Union is able to deliver on the 2030 targets for renewable energy and energy efficiency improvements, and it must support the green transition and modernisation of the European economy at all. The European energy systems and energy markets are now closely linked together. The winter package can therefore help to pave the way for energy to flow more freely across borders within the European Union. It is also the Government's hope that the winter package will help to boost the development of an internal electricity market. Here, the Nordic Region must be a role model for Europe in order to illustrate how to ensure cost-effective integration of large amounts of renewable energy. This should be done through extensive trade across borders, where we benefit from each other's resources. On the more domestic levels, the Energy Commission published its recommendations on the energy policy of the future on 24 April this year. This is a highly qualified and ambitious piece of work, which represents a significant contribution to the government's proposal for a new energy agreement that will be presented in autumn 2017. In order to ensure that the transition to the low-mission society is carried out in a sensible way, a paradigm change is needed in the way we conduct energy policy today in Denmark. I am particularly sensitive to three recommendations from the Energy Commission. Firstly, the market must play a greater role. The Energy Commission recommends that we can no longer politically identify one particular technology and one fixed path from here to 2030. On the other hand, we must put some pointers and steer them by following developments and allowing the market to offer the right solutions so that we can have as much green conversion as possible for the money. Secondly, I am sensitive to the Commission's recommendation that we should be better at using our green energy. There is a need for increased electrification in e.g. the heating sector. Thirdly, I agree with the Energy Commission that it is sensible to look at energy taxes. Energy taxes today provide approx. 40 billion. kr. in the Treasury a year, so it's not an easy exercise like that to turn it all upside down. The Energy Commission's recommendations have an important role to play in relation to the forthcoming Energy Agreement. As you probably already know, the government will present a proposal for a new broad energy agreement in the autumn, and here the recommendations will include: form the basis for parts of the playout. Of course, a new energy agreement must also play up to the government's 2030 target of at least 50%. renewable energy. I can't wait to present the show this autumn. I cannot go into detail about the content at this stage, but the paper must contribute to the government's overall vision that Denmark should have the most integrated, market-based and flexible energy system in Europe. It must be an energy system that can handle increasing volumes of renewable energy in a cost-effective way while maintaining one of the highest levels of security of supply and lowest wholesale electricity prices in Europe. On the whole, I think it is essential that we help renewable energy sources to a place where they do not need any support at all. One of the ways we do this is to ensure that there is competition between technologies. As it is today, we do not really know the real price of land wind, for example. I know that you and many others are eagerly waiting for what will replace the current rural wind farm subsidies, but I still cannot lift the veil of a concrete model. It is a work that requires thoroughness and it is extensive, and at the same time it will have significant consequences for the way in which renewable energy is supported, and it requires us to be thorough in our work on it. However, I am working to present a proposal before the summer recess, and it is therefore still my intention that we should get on with a clarification before we have to negotiate a new energy settlement. So I do not yet have a complete model in place, but I can say so much that in the new model there must be competition between the technologies, and this could well be a form of technology-neutral supply. Although, of course, this is the way we go, Danish energy policy is also much more than just renewable energy. As you know, we also have a good oil and gas industry. which is very much alive in the North Sea. On 22 March 2017, the Government concluded a political agreement with the Social Democratic Party, the Danish People's Party, the SF and the Radical Left on the development of the North Sea. At the same time, on 23 March 2017, the Government entered into an agreement with A.P. Møller - Mærsk A/S and Mærsk Oil and Gas on behalf of the DUC on the full reconstruction of the facility on the Tyra field. The political agreement contains concessions which, on the one hand, make it more profitable for DUC to rebuild Tyra. On the other hand, demands are made, for example. on improving access for third parties to central infrastructure. This will benefit the whole sector and ensure the efficient use of resources in the North Sea. In addition, the agreement also contains a pool for experiments with more environmentally friendly and energy-efficient production of oil and gas. Now the agreement needs to be followed up. The long-awaited oil and gas strategy will be published in cooperation with the industry and the agreement's requirements for improved third-party departure must be implemented in the legislation. Finally, I would like to conclude with a few cuts in the core areas of energy policy. The energy policy statement is, of course, a review of them, and I would like to highlight a few. Winning bid on Krieger's Flak with 37.2 cents per. kilowatt-time was a great world record at the time of the bidding. The winds of the sea continue to exceed all expectations of the price, and it is exciting what it can become. In December, the pilot offer of solar cells was settled with a fine bid. The offer gave a subsidy price of 12.89 cents per year. kilowatt-hour, which is given as a fixed price surcharge on top of the market price for 20 years. It is significantly cheaper than the aid schemes that have otherwise been. I believe that both pilot and Kriegers Flak show how supply is a good mechanism to drive down the price of renewable energy. Many of you in this Chamber also helped to conclude a historic PSP agreement in November last year. The agreement abolishes the PSO tax and transfers the cost of renewable energy to the Finance Act. The abolition of the PSO tax is done in a way that strengthens Danish business and is socially balanced. It will also be cheaper for both Danish households and companies. I would like to conclude by mentioning what I think are two beautiful features of the Danish energy policy, namely that it is ambitious and that it has broad support. It is important that the energy policy of the future has the same characteristics. We must ensure the broad consensus and thus ensure that we achieve the cost-effective transformation, which is a guiding force for government policy and a policy that is absolutely possible. I and the government will work to create a new broad energy policy agreement for the period after 2020 during this parliamentary term. In energy and climate policy, long-term and stable framework conditions are important. It seems to me that it is essential to maintain Denmark's green leadership that we get an agreement in place in good time by 2020 on how energy policy will look after 2020. There are almost 60,000 Danish jobs and 84 billion. kr. in play on exports, and I am in work clothes and very much hope that the rest of the Danish Parliament will agree with me that it will be timely care to have a new broad energy agreement made in good time by 2020. I look forward to the dialogue on a new agreement when the government delivers its proposal in the autumn, and now I look forward to hearing the dialogue in this Chamber. Thank you for the floor and good debate.

We simply need not only to produce renewable energy as we are today, but also to be better at using renewable energy. This requires electrification; it requires us to invest massively in a more flexible system that can handle renewable and fluctuating energy; and it requires us to invest in, for example, storage technology, so that we can, in popular terms, save wind power for the days when it doesn't blow. We simply have to get into a situation where we use more of the green stream and not just produce more.
