ParlaMint-DK_20170523211127	The debate is open. And the first to speak here is the Minister for Energy, Supply and Climate.
ParlaMint-DK_20170523211141	Thank you. I thank the Enhedslisten for the proposal. First of all, I would like to make it clear that this is not a proposal that the government can support. At the same time, however, I would like to acknowledge that the Enhedslisten with the proposal touches on an important point which has the attention of the government. The Enhedslisten proposes that district heating companies be required to provide information on their use of biomass, including on the type of biomass used, as well as on whether the biomass is covered by the voluntary trade agreement. The proposal also requires the same information to be incorporated into the annual energy statistics at aggregated national level. The Enhedslisten is absolutely right that Denmark, like a number of other EU countries, is increasingly using biomass in the energy supply and that emissions from combustion are considered as CO2 But Enhedslisten is not right in that this approach is not related to reality. Denmark follows guidelines from the UN Climate Panel when preparing the national greenhouse gas emissions accounts. The logic is that greenhouse gas emissions from biomass are included when the biomass is removed from e.g. the forest or the field. The emissions that come from the entire cultivation process, for example. fertilization and transport are included in the agricultural and transport sector surveys in the countries where they take place. Reporting to the UN and the EU is to create a comprehensive and full overview of global emissions and to be able to follow the development of each country's emissions and here it is important that the same emissions are not counted twice. The government is fully aware that the biomass used not only for district heating but also for electricity generation must be sustainable. The government basically believes that rules for sustainable biomass should be established at EU level in order to create a level playing field for electricity and heat producers across the EU. Similarly, the government believes that the global inventory of greenhouse gas emissions should be based on common principles established within the framework of the United Nations. It should also be mentioned in this context that the CO2 Government welcomes the fact that, as part of the clean energy package, also known as the winter package, the European Commission has put forward proposals for common European sustainability criteria for all types of bioenergy, including solid biomass. The common European regulation is not ready tomorrow, and that is why I am very pleased that the industry has concluded the voluntary trade agreement which ensures that the use of biomass for energy production also leads to real and documented greenhouse gas reductions. In view of the fact that, as I said, the government is working for common international solutions, which should apply after 2020, we do not believe that it is appropriate to establish comprehensive national legislation at this stage. It is worth noting that the industry agreement already contains an obligation to provide information, and this obligation implies that district heating companies must publish reports on their websites on the use of biomass, which demonstrates how they comply with the sustainability criteria of the industry agreement. Finally, let me thank you once again for the proposal. I hope that today's discussions make it clear that when the government does not support the Enhedslisten's proposal, it is not because we disagree with the need to ensure that the biomass we use for energy production is truly sustainable, on the contrary, and therefore we also support the voluntary trade agreement and work for common European sustainability criteria that will apply for the period after 2020. However, we do not believe that a new national detailed regulation on the obligation to provide information will contribute further to addressing the challenges. On the other hand, I will work to ensure that Denmark, in the European Union and in cooperation with the industry, can contribute to creating the best possible conditions for the sustainable use of biomass.

ParlaMint-DK_20170523213630	I am very sorry, but sometimes things go a little bit fast in the Parliamentary Chamber; sometimes things go slow, and sometimes things go fast. But now I have found the papers and we are going to deal today with a motion for a resolution from the Enhedslisten, the Alternative and the Socialist People's Party, in which the parties are proposing to abolish tax advantages at parental buying apartments. I should like to start by saying that the government is generally positive about proposals that support a fairer and balanced tax system, and I therefore recognise that the intention behind the proposal can be good enough in terms of taxation, but I do not believe that the proposal is the right one to ensure the balance that is demanded. That is why the government intends to reject the motion for a resolution. Firstly, the proposal seeks to prevent taxation under the rules of the Corporate Tax Act for the rental of parents' apartments. As a justification, the parties behind the proposal refer to the fact that the opportunities for parental buying are largely available only for the wealthiest part of the population. I'm not so concerned about whether a trader is rich or poor. A successful trader can create lots of jobs that Denmark needs. However, I can of course support the fact that there should be no arbitrary access to the tax conditions that apply to the business of self-employed persons, such as the United Kingdom. business arrangements. At present, therefore, the Danish Parliament is considering a draft law tabled by the Government, entitled "L 194', which aims to ensure that the tax conditions intended to apply to real business enterprises are precisely targeted at such companies. In my view and in the view of the government, the proposed intervention contains a number of problems, which is why we cannot support it. The proposal therefore requires that cases of so-called parents' purchase be identified in a legislative manner; that is to say, cases where parents buy an apartment for their child's rental. In order to hit those situations, it will require a detailed but also too vulnerable definition of the personal relationships to be covered by the proposal, and in practice such rules will be difficult, almost impossible to manage and will require additional control activities from SKAT, and they will also be relatively easy to deal with. In general, I have reservations about introducing legislation that is difficult to administer and can be easily circumvented. You have to be very careful about that. Overall, the Government does not consider that the purpose of targeting the application of the corporate scheme outweighs the administrative problems posed by the proposal and, on that basis, the Government cannot support the part of the bill relating to the abolition of the possibility of applying the company scheme to parents' apartments. As I said, it is because everyone has the opportunity to take advantage of what many parents use to make sure that their child or children have a home. Among other things: In those cities where it may be difficult to access rental accommodation, some people take advantage of this option. It is not an option that is aimed at parents, but part of the tax legislation that is particularly used to buy apartments from parents for children. That is why we cannot simply take action against the parent part. It would then have to be a general procedure. As I said, the government does not support that. Secondly, the motion for a resolution proposes the abolition of the so-called 15-percent rule, which can be applied to the transfer of property between related parties. The 15-percent rule has not normally been applied to parents' apartments, since the parents' apartments have been regarded as rental properties in terms of assessment. In other words, it has so far been the case that the commercial value of such transfers has had to be used. However, not so long ago we have reached a broad political agreement, which translates into a new property assessment system, and there are two points I would like to highlight in this context: the new system must restore confidence in public assessments and provide more accurate assessments of Danes' properties, and for reasons of legal certainty I believe that all Danes should be able to support the public assessment of their property. I must also acknowledge, however, that the valuation of real estate is linked to a certain degree of uncertainty, and that is precisely the uncertainty that justifies the closeness to determine the value of real estate within a certain range of public valuations. And with the new property valuations, the rental ratio is no longer to be taken into account and the property will thus be assessed at the commercial value of the property as free, and the transfer can take place as a starting point for the property valuation +/- 20 per cent. This corresponds to the uncertainty margin within which a general assessment may lie, without it being wrong for that reason. The upcoming property valuation system will therefore support future real estate transfer at the right value, knowing that the real value is a spread. Against this background, the government cannot support that part of the resolution which concerns the 15-percent rule. Therefore, overall, as the government cannot support either of the two key parts of the resolution, I must inform you that the government does not support the motion for a resolution tabled.

ParlaMint-DK_20170523224851	The debate is open. I give the floor to the Minister of Taxation.
ParlaMint-DK_20170523224915	Thank you, President. This evening we are debating a motion for a resolution from the Alternative on the reduction of VAT on fruit and vegetables. Specifically, the alternative proposes that VAT should be reduced from 25% as it stands today to 121⁄2%. on fruit and vegetables. It is not long since we discussed a similar motion for a resolution from the Alternative, namely B 69, which dealt with a reduction in VAT rates from 25%. 12%. on small repairs of bicycles, leather goods and textiles. Therefore, allow me to be a little brief, as the arguments for not introducing a reduced VAT rate go back, and it is perhaps not so surprising that they do so. It would be surprising if they didn't leave. So what are the arguments? These are, of course, the same arguments as when the motion for a resolution on small repairs was rejected. The Danish VAT system is, after all, characterised by the fact that all goods and services are initially subject to 25 per cent. VAT. Over time, changing governments have held on to this, and so there are many good reasons for doing so. And I believe that those reasons remain valid. What, then, are the reasons? It goes without saying that a single-rate VAT system is easier to administer than a VAT system with many different rates. After all, there are many who criticise the tax system for being complicated. I also think I have heard from the Alternative's tax rapporteur on one or two occasions that it has been pointed out that the tax system is becoming more complicated. There I have to say that differentiated VAT rates do not simplify the tax system. On the contrary, it is made more complicated. The ease with which a VAT rate is administered applies both to companies and to authorities, and the administrative problems with several VAT rates are due, among other things, to: problems of demarcation, given that there is greater uncertainty as to whether the product in question will now also be subject to the appropriate VAT rate. And administrative demarcation problems entail significant costs for business and also for authorities to ensure that the rules are complied with. What could it be? As an illustration, I can mention tomatoes. It is logical that fresh tomatoes should be included in the proposal, but what about canned tomatoes, dried tomatoes, dried tomatoes in oil, pured tomatoes, tomato concentrate, tomato salad and I can continue to do so should they also be subject to lower taxation? Where do you put the incision? And what is the professional justification for that cut? The increased costs of this administration will mean that a reduction in VAT will not be fully achieved by consumers, as companies will of course cover the additional administrative costs. A reduction in VAT on fruit and vegetables is also not a sensible instrument for promoting the consumption of fruit and vegetables. Why isn't it? This is because the reduced VAT on fruit and vegetables will largely benefit already existing consumption of fruit and vegetables. So there may be some vegetable traders who will be happy, but there is also some natural limit to how much fruit and vegetables are being sold and how much people can eat. This means that a reduction will mainly concern consumers' current consumption and thus only to a limited extent contribute to improving public health. This is not so good with the equality objective or equality weighting of the proposers. Now I have to find the right words, because this is a proposal that will be of great benefit to the wealthy consumers. It is they who have a high consumption of, among other things. organic fruit and vegetables, such as: will be part of the product group that will be included in this. In other words, the proposal for a relaxation is turning the heavy end down compared with the groups that belong more to those who are traditionally socially at risk. This will therefore only contribute to improving public health to a limited extent. In addition, a reduction in VAT will act as a price subsidy which is independent of the health of the product or the quantity in which the product is purchased. The same arguments apply if an improvement in the environment and climate is to be achieved. A price subsidy in the form of reduced VAT is not linked to the impact of the product on the environment and climate, which is why it must be considered an expensive way of improving the environment and climate. Differentiated VAT is therefore not the best or the most effective way of improving public health or the climate, which is, after all, a fair and legitimate view of the alternative and which they put forward in the resolution. In addition, the proposal entails an estimated annual financing requirement of 1.6 billion. kr. to be financed by other means. And the VAT revenue lost by the State because of a reduced VAT on fruit and vegetables will have to be collected elsewhere if the proposal is to be implemented. It may be on health, it may be on care for the elderly, it may also be through increased taxes, there are a number of options, but it must at least be financed. To sum up: the proposed halving of VAT on fruit and vegetables will not go through fully with the consumer; it will lead to major administrative problems both for business and for the tax administration and to an estimated financing requirement of 1.6 billion. kr., which will have to be financed by other means. And for these reasons, and certainly for a number of additional reasons, which I do not mention here because time does not allow it, the government cannot support the alternative's motion for a resolution to reduce VAT on fruit and vegetables.

ParlaMint-DK_20170523231124	Thank you very much. This Regulation shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union. It is proposed that it should be reduced from 25 per cent. 121⁄2%, which is the lowest possible rate in accordance with EU rules. I can follow the motivation to make fruit and vegetables cheaper for everyone and thus create better health for the Danes and reduce climate pressures. At the same time, however, we consider that the resolution will not have the effect that the alternative wants. After all, our VAT system is characterised by a single rate. The average Danish VAT is 25%. of the price of the goods. A system with a lower VAT rate on fruit and vegetables is both more complicated and more expensive to administer than a system with only one rate. And we already have a very complicated tax system with all the different excise duties in the world. It will also have an impact on both the companies selling the various goods and the authorities that check that the VAT rate should be applied to the right goods. And that will mean a great deal in particular to the retail trade. The objective of improving public health with the reduced VAT rate is not, in general, an effective approach. This will primarily benefit existing consumers, which means that to a very limited extent it will mean that people will actually eat more fruit and vegetables. Since we also know that those who eat a lot of fruit and vegetables are often the socially strong in a society, it will also be socially divided, because it will in particular be the socially strong who will then have a relief in tax. At the same time, the VAT reduction will be matched by increases in the pre-VAT price due to increased administrative costs for companies, and therefore the reduced VAT rate will not benefit the price and therefore the health of the Danes. That is why the Left cannot support the alternative proposal. And I should like to welcome the Conservatives and say that they cannot support the resolution either.
